## Acts 21:1 (id: 9482)

* Cos was an island in the Aegean Sea with a major trade port.
* Rhodes is a large Aegean island. The Colossus, a huge statue 30 meters (100 feet) tall, once stood at the city's entrance. In Paul’s time, the statue lay where it had fallen during an earthquake over 200 years earlier. It would remain there for another 600 years.
* Patara was the main port of Lycia, located on the coast across from Rhodes.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 21:1

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Cos (ID: `place:Cos`); Rhodes (ID: `place:Rhodes`); Patara (ID: `place:Patara`)

## Acts 21:2–3 (id: 9483)

Tyre was an important port in Phoenicia with an empire centered on sea activity and widespread trade interests (see [Isaiah 23](https://ref.ly/Isa23:1-Isa23:18); [Jeremiah 25:15–38](https://ref.ly/Jer25:15-Jer25:38); [47](https://ref.ly/Jer47:1-Jer47:7); [Zechariah 9](https://ref.ly/Zech9:1-Zech9:17); [Matthew 15:21–28](https://ref.ly/Matt15:21-Matt15:28); [Mark 7:24–31](https://ref.ly/Mark7:24-Mark7:31)).

## Acts 21:4–6 (id: 9491)

* The disciples in Tyre gave Paul a warm and emotional farewell. This shows the deep fellowship (shared life and unity) among Christians.
* *Through the Spirit they kept telling Paul not to go up to Jerusalem**:* The disciples in Tyre clearly understood the danger he would face there. Because they cared for him, they tried to persuade him not to go.

    However, Paul accepted these risks. He chose to go in order to complete the mission God had given him (compare [Philippians 3:7–10](https://ref.ly/Phil3:7-Phil3:10)). See also study note on Acts 21:11–14.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 21:4-6
* **Associated Articles:** Acts 21:11-14 (ID: `9480@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Be a Disciple (ID: `keyterm:Disciple`); Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Holy Spirit (ID: `deity:HolySpirit`); Pray (ID: `keyterm:Pray`); Ship (ID: `realia:Ship`)

## Acts 21:9 (id: 9494)

Philip’s daughters had the gift of prophecy (the ability to speak messages from God). This showed that Joel’s prophecy ([Joel 2:28–32](https://ref.ly/Joel2:28-Joel2:32)) had been fulfilled, just as Peter had said at Pentecost ([2:17–21](https://ref.ly/Acts2:17-Acts2:21)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 21:9

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Speak as a Prophet (ID: `keyterm:Speak`)

## Acts 21:10 (id: 9479)

Luke places female prophets ([21:9](https://ref.ly/Acts21:9)) alongside a male prophet (see study note on 17:34). This shows that both women and men were used by God to speak his message in the early church.

Agabus, like the Old Testament prophets (God’s messengers before the time of Jesus), used symbolic actions (visible actions that show a message) to communicate what God had revealed to him (see “Prophetic Sign Acts” Theme Note).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 21:10
* **Associated Articles:** Acts 17:34 (ID: `9413@TyndaleStudyNotes`); Prophetic Sign Acts (ID: `500313@TyndaleStudyNotesThemes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Agabus (ID: `person:Agabus`); Judea (ID: `place:Judea`); Name (ID: `keyterm:Name`)

## Acts 21:11–14 (id: 9480)

Despite Agabus’s prediction of suffering and the believers’ begging Paul not to go to Jerusalem, Paul was ready even to die for the sake of the Lord Jesus. Luke emphasizes Paul’s courage, determination, and heroism as a Christian missionary who would let nothing interfere with his mission. See study note on 21:4–6.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 21:11-14
* **Associated Articles:** Acts 21:4-6 (ID: `9491@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

## Acts 21:18–19 (id: 9481)

It was important for James and all the elders (leaders) of the church in Jerusalem to hear about Paul’s successful work among the Gentiles. It was also important for the Jerusalem church, the first main church, to continue to support this mission ([21:20](https://ref.ly/Acts21:20); see [15:7–21](https://ref.ly/Acts15:7-Acts15:21)). 

Paul’s report showed that God had carried out his plan among the Gentiles through Paul’s work.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 21:18-19

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** James (ID: `person:James.3`); Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Serve (ID: `keyterm:Serve`); Gentiles (ID: `group:Gentile`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Acts 21:20–25 (id: 9484)

The leaders of the Jerusalem church urged Paul to strengthen his credibility. They encouraged him to demonstrate that he was not teaching Jews to abandon the laws of Moses. Meanwhile, the leaders did not force Jewish rules on Gentile converts. They had determined these terms earlier and would respect them ([15:22–29](https://ref.ly/Acts15:22-Acts15:29)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 21:20-25

## Acts 21:26–36 (id: 9485)

The concerns of the Christian leaders were well\-founded ([21:20–25](https://ref.ly/Acts21:20-Acts21:25)). When Paul and the other men had almost completed their vow, some Jews from Asia falsely accused Paul and raised a mob against him. They intended to kill Paul, but the Roman commander rescued him.

## Acts 21:28–29 (id: 9486)

*Greeks*: It was a crime punishable by death to bring Gentiles (non\-Jewish people) into the inner areas of the temple, beyond the Court of the Gentiles (see also Josephus, *War* 5\.5\.2; 6\.2\.4\). The Jews believed Paul broke this holy law by bringing Trophimus, a Gentile from Ephesus, into the area reserved for only Jews (see also [Acts 20:4](https://ref.ly/Acts20:4); [2 Timothy 4:20](https://ref.ly/2Tim4:20)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 21:28-29

Herod the Great built the Antonia Fortress, a large military base, on the northwest corner of the Temple Mount. It housed the Roman soldiers stationed there to keep the peace.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 21:34

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Something Definite (ID: `keyterm:SomethingDefinite`)

## Acts 21:37–40 (id: 9490)

The commander had mistaken Paul for an Egyptian false messiah who had planned to seize power from the Romans around AD 54 (roughly three years earlier; see Josephus, *War* 2\.13\.5\). Paul corrected the mistake, gained permission to speak to the people, and addressed the crowd in Aramaic, the common language of Judea. He gave a strong statement of his faith in Jesus as the Messiah ([Acts 22:1–21](https://ref.ly/Acts22:1-Acts22:21)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 21:37-40

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Tarsus (ID: `place:Tarsus`); Cilicia (ID: `place:Cilicia`); Tarsians (ID: `group:Tarsus`); Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`); Jews (ID: `group:Jews`); Be Lawful (ID: `keyterm:Lawful`); Military Member (ID: `person:MilitaryMember`); Egyptians (ID: `group:Egypt`); Step (ID: `realia:Step`)

## Acts 22:1–21 (id: 9495)

Paul’s premier defense of his life and faith before his own people in Jerusalem illustrates his flexibility as a missionary, just as his speech to the Greek philosophers in Athens had done ([17:22–31](https://ref.ly/Acts17:22-Acts17:31); see [1 Cor 9:20–23](https://ref.ly/1Cor9:20-1Cor9:23)). Paul begins by recognizing his kinship with his people, explaining his Jewish background and training under the noted rabbi Gamaliel the Elder and describing his zealous desire to honor God in everything*,* which they shared ([Acts 22:1–3](https://ref.ly/Acts22:1-Acts22:3)). Paul then describes his persecution of Christians ([22:4–5](https://ref.ly/Acts22:4-Acts22:5)), the revelation of Jesus to him on the way to Damascus ([22:6–10](https://ref.ly/Acts22:6-Acts22:10)), and his conversion ([22:11–16](https://ref.ly/Acts22:11-Acts22:16)). Paul ends his speech by describing his conversation with the Lord in the Temple. The Lord had predicted the Jews’ rejection of the message and had sent Paul to the Gentiles ([22:17–21](https://ref.ly/Acts22:17-Acts22:21)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 22:1-21

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Damascus (ID: `place:Damascus`); Bear Witness (ID: `keyterm:BearWitness`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`); Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Jews (ID: `group:Jews`); Law (ID: `keyterm:Law`); Witness (ID: `keyterm:Witness`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Ananias (ID: `person:Ananias.2`); Gamaliel (ID: `person:Gamaliel`); Call Upon (ID: `keyterm:CallUpon`); Cilicia (ID: `place:Cilicia`); Baptism (ID: `keyterm:Baptism`); Blood (ID: `keyterm:Blood`); Glory (ID: `keyterm:Glory`); Tarsus (ID: `place:Tarsus`); Stephen (ID: `person:Stephen`); Be (Or Show Oneself) Just (ID: `keyterm:Just`); To Sin (ID: `keyterm:Sin`); Name (ID: `keyterm:Name`); Believe (ID: `keyterm:Believe`); Sky (ID: `keyterm:Heaven`); Pray (ID: `keyterm:Pray`); Nazareth (ID: `place:Nazareth`); Gentiles (ID: `group:Gentile`); The Way (ID: `keyterm:TheWay`); Prison (ID: `realia:Prison`); Cloak (ID: `realia:Cloak`); Synagogue (ID: `realia:Synagogue`); Temple (ID: `realia:Temple`)

## Acts 22:17–22 (id: 9499)

While Paul was praying in the temple*,* he saw a vision of Jesus. Jesus told him that the people of Jerusalem would not accept his message. The Lord then sent him to the Gentiles. The crowd’s reaction to this report confirmed this reality ([22:22–23](https://ref.ly/Acts22:22-Acts22:23)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 22:17-22

## Acts 22:23 (id: 9500)

The crowd’s yelling, throwing off their coats*,* and tossing handfuls of dust into the air were probably ritual responses to perceived blasphemy. They opposed and tried to thwart Paul’s words that suggested the inclusion of Gentiles ([22:21](https://ref.ly/Acts22:21)). Cp. [Luke 4:16–30](https://ref.ly/Luke4:16-Luke4:30).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 22:23

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Cloak (ID: `realia:Cloak`)

## Acts 22:25–29 (id: 9501)

Paul claimed his status as a Roman citizen at this critical time when he was about to be tortured to make him confess his supposed crime. Roman citizenship was a valuable asset, and claiming it falsely was a capital offense. Its principal benefits were the prohibition of scourging and the right to appeal to the emperor ([25:11](https://ref.ly/Acts25:11)). The commander was frightened at having nearly violated Roman law (cp. [16:35–39](https://ref.ly/Acts16:35-Acts16:39)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 22:25-29

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Rome (ID: `place:Rome`); Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Military Member (ID: `person:MilitaryMember`); Be Lawful (ID: `keyterm:Lawful`); Fear (ID: `keyterm:Fear`); Strap (ID: `realia:Strap`)

## Acts 22:28 (id: 9502)

it cost me plenty! During the early part of the reign of Emperor Claudius (AD 41–54\), Roman citizenship could be purchased, but it was expensive.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 22:28

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Military Member (ID: `person:MilitaryMember`); Paul (ID: `person:Paul`)

## Acts 23:1 (id: 9507)

In addressing the high council, Paul insisted on his personal integrity before God—he had not violated God’s law or done the things they accused him of doing.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 23:1

Ananias was the Jewish high priest from AD 47 to 58\. • slap him: He apparently assumed that Paul was lying and tried to intimidate him.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 23:2

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Ananias (ID: `person:Ananias.3`)

## Acts 23:3 (id: 9514)

*you whitewashed wall*: Compare [Ezekiel 13:10–17](https://ref.ly/Ezek13:10-Ezek13:17); [Matthew 23:27](https://ref.ly/Matt23:27).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 23:3

## Acts 23:5 (id: 9515)

* It is unclear why Paul did not recognize the high priest.
* Paul responded by quoting [Exodus 22:28](https://ref.ly/Exod22:28), showing respect for the high priest's position.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 23:5

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Paul (ID: `person:Paul`)

## Acts 23:6 (id: 9516)

Paul focused on the key issue in his trial, the hope of resurrection from the dead. His preaching was simply the outworking of that hope and the fact of Jesus’ resurrection, but the message was unacceptable to both groups of Jews because of its implications. Pharisees could not abide the inclusion of the Gentiles apart from circumcision and keeping the law of Moses (cp. [15:5](https://ref.ly/Acts15:5)), but that was what the resurrection of Jesus and the outpouring of the Spirit had provided ([Acts 2:39](https://ref.ly/Acts2:39); [10:34–48](https://ref.ly/Acts10:34-Acts10:48)). Sadducees could not stand the proclamation of the resurrection at all (cp. [4:1–2](https://ref.ly/Acts4:1-Acts4:2)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 23:6

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Resurrection (ID: `keyterm:Resurrection`); Pharisees (ID: `group:Pharisee`); Sadducees (ID: `group:Sadducee`); Decided (ID: `keyterm:Decided`); Hope (ID: `keyterm:Hope`)

## Acts 23:7–10 (id: 9517)

Paul’s statement ([23:6](https://ref.ly/Acts23:6)) divided the council*,* with the Pharisees taking Paul’s side against the Sadducees. The resulting uproar was so great that the commander rescued Paul and took him back into the fortress of Antonia.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 23:7-10

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Pharisees (ID: `group:Pharisee`); An Angel (ID: `deity:Angel`); Sadducees (ID: `group:Sadducee`); Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Resurrection (ID: `keyterm:Resurrection`); Fear (ID: `keyterm:Fear`)

At this critical juncture, Jesus encouraged Paul to continue as his faithful witness by assuring him that he would go to Rome (see [19:21](https://ref.ly/Acts19:21)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 23:11

Paul’s nephew stopped the plan to kill him by telling a Roman officer.

## Acts 23:23–35 (id: 9508)

A large group riding horses safely brought Paul to the Roman governor Felix in Caesarea, the Roman headquarters for Judea. Paul would have more protection there than in Jerusalem. The soldiers carried out the mission that night with great care and secrecy ([23:31](https://ref.ly/Acts23:31)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 23:23-35

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Felix (ID: `person:Felix`); Caesarea (ID: `place:Caesarea`); Cilicia (ID: `place:Cilicia`); Lysias (ID: `person:Lysias`); Antipatris (ID: `place:Antipatris`); Rome (ID: `place:Rome`); Herod (ID: `person:Herod`); Jews (ID: `group:Jews`); Salvation (ID: `keyterm:Salvation`); Law (ID: `keyterm:Law`); Rescue (ID: `keyterm:Rescue`); Palace (ID: `realia:Palace`)

## Acts 23:24 (id: 9509)

Antonius Felix was the governor of Judea from about AD 52 to 59\. He was in charge of both military and civil matters. Felix had a bad reputation and was eventually called back to Rome by Emperor Nero (see [24:24–27](https://ref.ly/Acts24:24-Acts24:27)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 23:24

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Felix (ID: `person:Felix`); Salvation (ID: `keyterm:Salvation`); Paul (ID: `person:Paul`)

## Acts 23:26–30 (id: 9510)

The letter of Claudius Lysias to Governor Felix is a typical Hellenistic letter, naming the writer and the person addressed, offering a greeting, and stating the business at hand (though there is no parting word of farewell; see study note on 15:29). It summarizes the events that preceded it and explains the action taken; it also suggests that the commander has followed proper Roman judicial procedure. • The title his Excellency was often applied to persons of high social, political, or economic status ([Luke 1:3](https://ref.ly/Luke1:3)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 23:26-30
* **Associated Articles:** Acts 15:29 (ID: `9373@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Lysias (ID: `person:Lysias`); Rome (ID: `place:Rome`); Jews (ID: `group:Jews`); Law (ID: `keyterm:Law`); Felix (ID: `person:Felix`); Rescue (ID: `keyterm:Rescue`)

## Acts 23:31 (id: 9512)

Antipatris*,* a city rebuilt by Herod the Great in 9 BC on the Plain of Sharon, was a convenient military control point between Jerusalem and Caesarea.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 23:31

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Antipatris (ID: `place:Antipatris`); Paul (ID: `person:Paul`)

## Acts 23:35 (id: 9513)

The governor followed the proper protocol and waited for Paul’s accusers to arrive before granting an official hearing. • Herod’s headquarters was Herod the Great’s palace at Caesarea; it subsequently became the residence of the Roman governors of Judea.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 23:35

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Palace (ID: `realia:Palace`); Herod (ID: `person:Herod`)

Tertullus presented the case for the prosecution, beginning with the customary speech of praise intended to attract the attention and sympathy of the governor, followed by the statement of charges ([24:5–8](https://ref.ly/Acts24:5-Acts24:8)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 24:1-4

## Acts 24:1–27 (id: 9518)

Tertullus presented a legal case against Paul in a Roman court on behalf of the high priest ([24:1–9](https://ref.ly/Acts24:1-Acts24:9)). Then Paul cheerfully made his defense and defended his faith ([24:10–21](https://ref.ly/Acts24:10-Acts24:21)), and the governor adjourned the hearing without a decision and left Paul in prison for two years ([24:22–27](https://ref.ly/Acts24:22-Acts24:27)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 24:1-27

## Acts 24:5 (id: 9531)

Troublemaker (or *agitator*) was a charge of political sedition. A Roman court would have taken this charge very seriously (see [16:21](https://ref.ly/Acts16:21); [17:7](https://ref.ly/Acts17:7); [18:13](https://ref.ly/Acts18:13)). Similar accusations were made against Jesus before Pilate ([Luke 23:2](https://ref.ly/Luke23:2), [5](https://ref.ly/Luke23:5), [14](https://ref.ly/Luke23:14)). • The term cult (or *party*, or *sect*) is used here in a negative sense to put Paul’s religion under a pallor of suspicion if not illegality (see also [Acts 24:14](https://ref.ly/Acts24:14)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 24:5

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jews (ID: `group:Jews`); Ability (ID: `keyterm:Ability`); Nazareth (ID: `place:Nazareth`)

The fear of the “coming day of judgment” unnerved Felix in a subsequent conversation with Paul ([24:25](https://ref.ly/Acts24:25)).

## Acts 24:16 (id: 9523)

Paul stressed that he had acted with a clear conscience (see [20:27](https://ref.ly/Acts20:27), [33](https://ref.ly/Acts20:33); [23:1](https://ref.ly/Acts23:1); [1 Cor 4:4](https://ref.ly/1Cor4:4); [2 Cor 1:12](https://ref.ly/2Cor1:12); [4:2](https://ref.ly/2Cor4:2); see also [2 Tim 1:3](https://ref.ly/2Tim1:3)). He had not departed from his Jewish heritage ([Acts 24:14](https://ref.ly/Acts24:14)), and he had no fear of God’s judgment ([24:15](https://ref.ly/Acts24:15)).

Proper treatment of a Roman citizen involved giving him some freedom and allowing his friends to visit and care for him.

## Acts 24:24 (id: 9527)

Drusilla was the sister of Herod Agrippa II and Bernice ([25:13](https://ref.ly/Acts25:13)); Drusilla had abandoned her former husband, Azizus the King of Emessa, and married Felix. Drusilla was Jewish*,* so in forsaking her original husband and marrying Felix she had disregarded God’s commands ([Exod 20:14](https://ref.ly/Exod20:14); [Deut 5:18](https://ref.ly/Deut5:18); cp. [Mal 2:16](https://ref.ly/Mal2:16); [Mark 10:12](https://ref.ly/Mark10:12)). Josephus criticized her for this (*Antiquities* 20\.7\.1–2\). Drusilla and her husband were confronted in their conversation with Paul by the prospect of judgment.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 24:24

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Drusilla (ID: `person:Drusilla`); Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`); Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Jews (ID: `group:Jews`); Believe (ID: `keyterm:Believe`); Felix (ID: `person:Felix`)

## Acts 24:25 (id: 9528)

Paul’s words about righteousness*,* self\-control*,* and coming divine judgment frightened Felix*,* who was notably corrupt ([24:26–27](https://ref.ly/Acts24:26-Acts24:27); see study notes on 23:24; 24:24).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 24:25

## Acts 24:26–27 (id: 9529)

Felix kept Paul in custody hoping for a bribe. When this failed and his own term ended, he left Paul in prison to gain favor with the Jewish people.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 24:26-27

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Festus (ID: `person:Festus`); Felix (ID: `person:Felix`); Favor (ID: `keyterm:Favor`); Jews (ID: `group:Jews`); Hope (ID: `keyterm:Hope`); Money (ID: `realia:Money`)

## Acts 24:27–25.5 (id: 9530)

Felix’s successor was Porcius Festus*,* appointed by Nero to be governor of Judea about AD 59\~62\. Josephus describes Festus as a conscientious, honest administrator who was not able to stem the rising tide of Jewish unrest despite his strong action against the party of the Assassins (Josephus, *Antiquities* 20\.8\.10; cp. [Acts 21:38](https://ref.ly/Acts21:38)). He resisted the Jewish leaders’ attempt to have Paul’s trial moved to Jerusalem, but he was not immune to their pressure ([25:9](https://ref.ly/Acts25:9)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 24:27-25:5

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Festus (ID: `person:Festus`); Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Caesarea (ID: `place:Caesarea`); Favor (ID: `keyterm:Favor`); Jews (ID: `group:Jews`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Felix (ID: `person:Felix`); Ask for (ID: `keyterm:AskFor`)

## Acts 25:7 (id: 9540)

The serious accusations were likely the ones mentioned earlier ([21:27–28](https://ref.ly/Acts21:27-Acts21:28); [24:5–9](https://ref.ly/Acts24:5-Acts24:9)). But there was no evidence to support them ([24:10–13](https://ref.ly/Acts24:10-Acts24:13)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 25:7

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Jews (ID: `group:Jews`)

## Acts 25:9 (id: 9541)

Festus, wanting to please the Jews: The governor would later state his official reason for delaying Paul’s trial and asking to move it to Jerusalem ([25:17–20](https://ref.ly/Acts25:17-Acts25:20)). His request was driven by politics, however, not justice.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 25:9

## Acts 25:10–11 (id: 9533)

Festus’s suggestion that Paul be tried in Jerusalem motivated the apostle’s appeal to Caesar. Paul was not afraid of death, but he objected to being turned over to a biased court intent on murder, not justice ([25:7](https://ref.ly/Acts25:7)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 25:10-11

Festus discussed Paul’s case with Herod Agrippa II (ruled AD 50–100\), who had come to Caesarea to make a courtesy call on the new governor.

## Acts 25:16 (id: 9536)

It was a fundamental principle: Roman law did not convict people without a trial. They must be given an opportunity to confront their accusers and defend themselves (cp. [19:38–39](https://ref.ly/Acts19:38-Acts19:39)). This put Roman judicial procedure in a favorable light for readers of Acts.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 25:16

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Rome (ID: `place:Rome`); Forgive (ID: `keyterm:Forgive`)

## Acts 25:17 (id: 9537)

*I did not delay*: This is different from Felix, the previous governor, who delayed making a decision ([24:22–27](https://ref.ly/Acts24:22-Acts24:27)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 25:17

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Judgment Seat (ID: `realia:JudgmentSeat`)

## Acts 25:18–20 (id: 9538)

These verses give Festus’s official reasons for his actions; see also [25:9](https://ref.ly/Acts25:9). There were no criminal accusations against Paul; the objections were about their religion and centered on Paul’s claim that Jesus . . . is alive.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 25:18-20

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`); Life (ID: `keyterm:Life`); Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Decided (ID: `keyterm:Decided`)

## Acts 25:23–27 (id: 9539)

Paul’s hearing before King Agrippa was accompanied by all the pomp and circumstance appropriate for an official royal visit. The main purpose of the hearing was for Agrippa to advise Festus on what he should write in the appeal to Caesar, for there was no clear charge against Paul, and Festus himself believed Paul had done nothing deserving death.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 25:23-27

## Acts 26:1–23 (id: 9542)

In his eloquent defense before King Agrippa*,* Paul argued that his preaching was completely consistent with the Jewish faith. The defense begins with a courteous acknowledgement of Agrippa’s competence to hear the evidence ([26:2–3](https://ref.ly/Acts26:2-Acts26:3)), outlines the nature of Paul’s background, Jewish training, and membership in the Pharisees ([26:4–5](https://ref.ly/Acts26:4-Acts26:5)), and explains that the charges against him are merely for believing the fulfillment of Jewish hopes for the resurrection ([26:6–8](https://ref.ly/Acts26:6-Acts26:8)). Paul then tells the story of his conversion from strong opponent of Christianity ([26:9–11](https://ref.ly/Acts26:9-Acts26:11)) through a vision on the way to Damascus ([26:12–18](https://ref.ly/Acts26:12-Acts26:18); see [9:1–18](https://ref.ly/Acts9:1-Acts9:18)). His preaching was nothing more than obeying this divine vision ([26:19–20](https://ref.ly/Acts26:19-Acts26:20)). Even though he encountered violent opposition from his fellow Jews ([26:21](https://ref.ly/Acts26:21)), God protected him as he taught a message that the Jews should have embraced ([26:22–23](https://ref.ly/Acts26:22-Acts26:23)). This defense is a model for Christians put on trial for their faith (see [9:15](https://ref.ly/Acts9:15); [Luke 21:12–15](https://ref.ly/Luke21:12-Luke21:15)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 26:1-23

## Acts 26:27–28 (id: 9548)

Paul’s question put Agrippa in a bind: If he said he believed the prophets*,* he knew Paul would press home the Christian message; if not, he would offend the devout Jews in his audience. Agrippa knew that Paul wasn’t crazy and that Paul’s testimony about Jesus was historically sound ([26:26](https://ref.ly/Acts26:26)). So Agrippa evaded Paul’s question and refused to face the claims of Christ, alleging that the statement given by Paul was too brief for him to arrive at a responsible decision.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 26:27-28

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Agrippa (ID: `person:Agrippa`); Christians (ID: `group:Christ`); Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`); Persuade (ID: `keyterm:Persuade`); Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Believe (ID: `keyterm:Believe`)

## Acts 26:28 (id: 9549)

“Do you think you can persuade me to become a Christian so quickly?”: This enigmatic remark might have been ironic, incredulous, scoffing, or brushing off Paul’s challenge. It also might have been a direct statement of Paul’s persuasiveness, or a direct statement about or genuine question of Paul’s intention. It seems best to take Agrippa’s reply as deliberately evasive: He didn’t want to admit that he believed the prophets ([26:27](https://ref.ly/Acts26:27)), for Paul had just made a strong case, and the next step would be to believe in Jesus as the promised Messiah to whom the prophets pointed. Agrippa didn’t want to take that step. On the other hand, he didn’t want to say that he didn’t believe the prophets, for that would alienate the Jewish subjects to whose loyalties he wanted to appeal. His non\-committal response underlines his discomfort with Paul’s testimony.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 26:28

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Christians (ID: `group:Christ`); Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`); Persuade (ID: `keyterm:Persuade`); Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Agrippa (ID: `person:Agrippa`)

## Acts 27:1–28.16 (id: 9553)

* The graphic language describing sailing in the story of Paul's journey to Rome provides one of the best accounts of an ancient sea journey.
* This is the last "we" section in Acts (see also [16:10–17](https://ref.ly/Acts16:10-Acts16:17); [20:5–15](https://ref.ly/Acts20:5-Acts20:15); [21:1–18](https://ref.ly/Acts21:1-Acts21:18)). ## Acts 28:1 (id: 9581)

Malta was a large island controlled by the Romans, located about 100 kilometers (60 miles) south of Sicily.

## Acts 28:3–6 (id: 9587)

The people of Malta believed that justice was a personal power or a god who punished criminals. When nothing bad happened to Paul, they changed their minds. They saw that he was not harmed by the snake. So they thought he had power over snakes and said that he must be a god (compare [14:11–12](https://ref.ly/Acts14:11-Acts14:12)).

But this was not true. Paul did not have special power in himself. Instead, his survival showed that God was protecting him (compare [Mark 16:17–18](https://ref.ly/Mark16:17-Mark16:18)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 28:3-6

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Be (Or Show Oneself) Just (ID: `keyterm:Just`); Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Life (ID: `keyterm:Life`); Salvation (ID: `keyterm:Salvation`); Snake (ID: `fauna:Snake`); Cobra (ID: `fauna:Cobra`)

## Acts 28:13–14 (id: 9577)

* They sailed across the Straits of Messina to Rhegium at the southern tip of Italy. This seaport was a stop for ships traveling from Italy's west coast to the eastern Mediterranean.
* Puteoli (modern Pozzuoli) was a major entry port for large ships bringing grain and supplies from the east to Rome. Paul spent a week here with local Christians before continuing to Rome.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 28:13-14

## Acts 28:16 (id: 9579)

They allowed Paul to have private quarters, with a soldier to guard him. Even though Paul traveled in chains, “the word of God cannot be chained!” ([2 Timothy 2:9](https://ref.ly/2Tim2:9)). Paul was possibly treated well because of his social status or Roman citizenship (compare [Acts 16:37–38](https://ref.ly/Acts16:37-Acts16:38); [22:25–28](https://ref.ly/Acts22:25-Acts22:28)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 28:16

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Rome (ID: `place:Rome`); Paul (ID: `person:Paul`)

## Acts 28:17–20 (id: 9580)

Conscious that the Good News was to be presented to the Jews first ([13:46](https://ref.ly/Acts13:46); [Rom 1:16](https://ref.ly/Rom1:16)) and concerned that the false charges against him might already have reached Rome, Paul summoned the local Jewish leaders and gave an account of his life and work. He insisted that he was guilty of no criminal offense, but strong Jewish opposition had made it necessary for him to appeal to the emperor. Paul had nothing against his own people*;* rather, he wanted to explain his great conviction that the Messiah they had been expecting had already come in the person of Jesus of Nazareth.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 28:17-20

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jews (ID: `group:Jews`); Rome (ID: `place:Rome`); Call Upon (ID: `keyterm:CallUpon`); Claudius (ID: `person:Claudius`); Discern (ID: `keyterm:Discern`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Ask for (ID: `keyterm:AskFor`); Hope (ID: `keyterm:Hope`); Chain (ID: `realia:Chain`)

## Acts 28:21–22 (id: 9582)

The Jewish leaders assured Paul that they had received no reports against him, and they wanted to hear his explanation of this movement.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 28:21-22

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Judea (ID: `place:Judea`); Think (ID: `keyterm:Think`)

## Acts 28:23 (id: 9583)

Paul explained how Jesus fulfilled the Old Testament hopes for the Kingdom of God*,* the master theme of Jesus’ own preaching ([Mark 1:14–15](https://ref.ly/Mark1:14-Mark1:15); see [Matt 4:12–17](https://ref.ly/Matt4:12-Matt4:17); [Luke 4:14–21](https://ref.ly/Luke4:14-Luke4:21), [43](https://ref.ly/Luke4:43)). Referring to the Scriptures*,* Paul presented the case for Jesus as the promised Messiah.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 28:23

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Persuade (ID: `keyterm:Persuade`); Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`); Law (ID: `keyterm:Law`); Moses (ID: `person:Moses`); Kingdom (ID: `keyterm:Kingdom`); Declare (ID: `keyterm:Declare`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Guest Room (ID: `realia:GuestRoom`)

## Acts 28:24 (id: 9584)

Paul spoke to them all day, but the people responded in different ways, just as people had done in other places (for example, [13:40–51](https://ref.ly/Acts13:40-Acts13:51); [17:11–14](https://ref.ly/Acts17:11-Acts17:14)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 28:24

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Persuade (ID: `keyterm:Persuade`); Disbelieve (ID: `keyterm:Disbelieve`)

## Acts 28:25–28 (id: 9585)

As Paul left, he gave a warning from Scripture. These words are often used in the New Testament to explain why many Jews did not accept the good news about Jesus (compare [Matthew 13:14–15](https://ref.ly/Matt13:14-Matt13:15); [Mark 4:12](https://ref.ly/Mark4:12); [Luke 8:10](https://ref.ly/Luke8:10); [John 12:38–40](https://ref.ly/John12:38-John12:40); see [Romans 11:1–12](https://ref.ly/Rom11:1-Rom11:12), [25–32](https://ref.ly/Rom11:25-Rom11:32)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 28:25-28

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Word (ID: `keyterm:Word`); Salvation (ID: `keyterm:Salvation`); Isaiah (ID: `person:Isaiah`); Gentiles (ID: `group:Gentile`); Holy Spirit (ID: `deity:HolySpirit`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Heart (ID: `keyterm:Heart`)

## Acts 28:28 (id: 9586)

Since Jews everywhere had the chance to accept the faith, it was now time to offer this salvation to the Gentiles ([13:46](https://ref.ly/Acts13:46); see [Romans 1:16](https://ref.ly/Rom1:16)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 28:28

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Gentiles (ID: `group:Gentile`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Salvation (ID: `keyterm:Salvation`)

## Acts 28:31 (id: 9588)

Despite being under house arrest ([28:16](https://ref.ly/Acts28:16)), Paul boldly proclaimed the Kingdom message. • And no one tried to stop him (Greek akōlutōs, “without hindrance”): This single word in Greek is the last word of the book of Acts and one of the keys to its meaning: God’s word cannot be chained, even when its messengers are ([2 Tim 2:9](https://ref.ly/2Tim2:9); see [Phil 1:12–14](https://ref.ly/Phil1:12-Phil1:14)). Acts is the story of an unhindered message of Good News, available to all people throughout the world, whether Jew, Gentile, proselyte, rich, or poor. The mission of proclaiming this message is accomplished in the power of the Spirit ([Acts 1:8](https://ref.ly/Acts1:8)); it embraces Jews ([3:1–5:42](https://ref.ly/Acts3:1-Acts5:42)), Samaritans ([8:1–25](https://ref.ly/Acts8:1-Acts8:25)), converts to Judaism ([2:11](https://ref.ly/Acts2:11); [13:43](https://ref.ly/Acts13:43)), “God\-fearers” ([8:26–40](https://ref.ly/Acts8:26-Acts8:40); [9:32–11:18](https://ref.ly/Acts9:32-Acts11:18)), and Gentiles ([13:1–28:28](https://ref.ly/Acts13:1-Acts28:28)). • Luke ends his account with Paul still under house arrest in Rome (about AD 60–62\). Paul was later released and traveled freely. According to tradition, Paul was imprisoned again in Rome about AD 64 and was martyred there during Nero’s persecution of believers.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 28:31

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Kingdom (ID: `keyterm:Kingdom`); Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`); Proclaim (ID: `keyterm:Proclaim`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)
